</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below" style="padding-top:0.1em;">
<a href="/wiki/File:P_christianity.svg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/16px-P_christianity.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="14" class="noviewer" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/24px-P_christianity.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/32px-P_christianity.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="360" /></a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Christianity" title="Portal:Christianity">Christianity&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar" title="Template:Oriental Orthodox sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar" title="Template talk:Oriental Orthodox sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Oriental_Orthodox_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Athanasius I of Alexandria</b><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup> (c. 296–298 – 2 May 373), also called <b>Athanasius the Great</b>, <b>Athanasius the Confessor</b>, or, among <a href="/wiki/Copts" title="Copts">Coptic Christians</a>, <b>Athanasius the Apostolic</b>, was a <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">church father</a><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and the 20th <a href="/wiki/Pope_of_the_Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Pope of the Coptic Orthodox Church">pope of Alexandria</a> (as <b>Athanasius I</b>). His intermittent episcopacy spanned 45 years (c. 8 June 328 – 2 May 373), of which over 17 encompassed five exiles, when he was replaced on the order of four different <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Roman emperors</a>. Athanasius was a <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theologian</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Father</a>, the chief defender of <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinitarianism</a> against <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a>, and a noted <a href="/wiki/Roman_Egypt" title="Roman Egypt">Egyptian</a> Christian leader of the fourth century.
</p><p>Conflict with <a href="/wiki/Arius" title="Arius">Arius</a> and Arianism, as well as with successive Roman emperors, shaped Athanasius' career. In 325, at age 27, Athanasius began his leading role against the Arians as a deacon and assistant to Bishop <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_I_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Alexander I of Alexandria">Alexander of Alexandria</a> during the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a>. Roman Emperor <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine the Great</a> had convened the council in May–August 325 to address the Arian position that the <a href="/wiki/Son_of_God" title="Son of God">Son of God</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> of Nazareth, is of a distinct substance from the <a href="/wiki/God_in_Christianity" title="God in Christianity">Father</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Three years after that council, Athanasius succeeded his mentor as pope of Alexandria. In addition to the conflict with the Arians (including powerful and influential Arian churchmen led by <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Nicomedia" title="Eusebius of Nicomedia">Eusebius of Nicomedia</a>), he struggled against the Emperors Constantine, <a href="/wiki/Constantius_II" title="Constantius II">Constantius II</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julian_(emperor)" title="Julian (emperor)">Julian the Apostate</a> and <a href="/wiki/Valens" title="Valens">Valens</a>. He was known as <i><b>Athanasius Contra Mundum</b></i> (Latin for 'Athanasius Against the World').
</p><p>Nonetheless, within a few years of his death, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> called him the "Pillar of the Church". His writings were well regarded by subsequent Church fathers in the West and the East, who noted their devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Logos_(Christianity)" title="Logos (Christianity)">Word-become-man</a>, pastoral concern and interest in <a href="/wiki/Monasticism" title="Monasticism">monasticism</a>. Athanasius is considered one of the four great Eastern <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Doctors_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Doctors-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In his <a href="/wiki/Easter_letter" title="Easter letter">Easter letter</a> of 367, Athanasius was the first person to list the 27 books of the <a href="/wiki/Development_of_the_New_Testament_canon" title="Development of the New Testament canon">New Testament canon</a> that are in use today.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He is venerated as a saint in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> the Catholic Church,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Orthodox Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio,_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_(giardino)_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto,_4-July-2008.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg/170px-2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg/255px-2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg/340px-2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio%2C_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_%28giardino%29_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto%2C_4-July-2008.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1224" data-file-height="1632" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:2896_-_Catania_-_Cattedrale_-_G._Nicoli_-_S._Atanasio,_nella_%27%27Floretta%27%27_(giardino)_-_Foto_Giovanni_Dall%27Orto,_4-July-2008.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A statue of Athanasius in <a href="/wiki/Catania" title="Catania">Catania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a></div></div></div>
<p>Athanasius was born to a Christian family in <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-oca_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oca-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> or possibly the nearby <a href="/wiki/Nile_Delta" title="Nile Delta">Nile Delta</a> town of <a href="/wiki/Damanhur" title="Damanhur">Damanhur</a>, sometime between 293 and 298. The earlier date is sometimes assigned because of the maturity revealed in his two earliest treatises <i>Contra Gentes</i> (<i>Against the Heathens</i>) and <i>De Incarnatione</i> (<i>On the Incarnation</i>), which were likely written circa 318 before Arianism had begun to make itself felt, as those writings do not show an awareness of Arianism.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Cornelius Clifford places his birth no earlier than 296 and no later than 298, based on the fact that Athanasius indicates no first-hand recollection of the <a href="/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution" title="Diocletianic Persecution">Maximian persecution</a> of 303, which he suggests Athanasius would have remembered if he had been ten years old at the time. Secondly, the <i>Festal Epistles</i> state that the Arians had accused Athanasius, among other charges, of not having yet attained the canonical age (35) and thus could not have been properly ordained as patriarch of Alexandria in 328. The accusation must have seemed plausible.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The Orthodox Church places his year of birth around 297.<sup id="cite_ref-oca_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oca-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>His parents were wealthy enough to give him a fine secular education.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He was, nevertheless, clearly not a member of the Egyptian aristocracy.<sup id="cite_ref-Barnes_2001_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnes_2001-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Some Western scholars consider his command of <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>, in which he wrote most (if not all) of his surviving works, evidence that he may have been a <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a> born in <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>. Historical evidence, however, indicates that he was fluent in <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> as well, given the regions of Egypt where he preached.<sup id="cite_ref-Barnes_2001_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnes_2001-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Some surviving copies of his writings are in fact in Coptic, though scholars differ as to whether he wrote them in Coptic originally (which would make him the first patriarch to do so) or whether these were translations of writings originally in Greek.<sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Barnes_2001_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnes_2001-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Tyrannius_Rufinus" title="Tyrannius Rufinus">Rufinus</a> relates a story that as Bishop <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_I_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Alexander I of Alexandria">Alexander</a> stood by a window, he watched boys playing on the seashore below, imitating the ritual of Christian baptism. He sent for the children and discovered that one of the boys (Athanasius) had acted as bishop. After questioning Athanasius, Bishop Alexander informed him that the baptisms were genuine, as both the <a href="/wiki/Sacramental_matter_and_form" title="Sacramental matter and form">form and matter of the sacrament</a> had been performed through the recitation of the correct words and the administration of water, and that he must not continue to do this as those baptized had not been properly <a href="/wiki/Catechesis" title="Catechesis">catechized</a>. He invited Athanasius and his playfellows to prepare for clerical careers.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1930_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1930-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexandria was the most important trade centre in the empire during Athanasius's boyhood. Intellectually, morally, and politically—it epitomized the ethnically diverse Graeco-Roman world, even more than Rome or Constantinople, Antioch or Marseilles.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1930_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1930-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Its famous catechetical school, while sacrificing none of its famous passion for orthodoxy since the days of <a href="/wiki/Pantaenus" title="Pantaenus">Pantaenus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria" title="Clement of Alexandria">Clement of Alexandria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Dionysius_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Dionysius of Alexandria">Dionysius</a> and <a href="/wiki/Theognostus_of_Alexandria" title="Theognostus of Alexandria">Theognostus</a>, had begun to take on an almost secular character in the comprehensiveness of its interests and had counted influential pagans among its serious auditors.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Peter_I_of_Alexandria" title="Peter I of Alexandria">Peter of Alexandria</a>, the 17th archbishop of Alexandria, was martyred in 311 in the closing days of the <a href="/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution" title="Diocletianic Persecution">Great Persecution</a> and may have been one of Athanasius's teachers. His successor as bishop of Alexandria was <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_I_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Alexander I of Alexandria">Alexander of Alexandria</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/Sozomen" title="Sozomen">Sozomen</a>; "the Bishop Alexander 'invited Athanasius to be his commensal and secretary. He had been well educated, and was versed in grammar and rhetoric, and had already, while still a young man, and before reaching the episcopate, given proof to those who dwelt with him of his wisdom and acumen' ".(Soz., II, xvii)<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Athanasius' earliest work, <i>Against the Heathen – On the Incarnation</i> (written before 319), bears traces of Origenist Alexandrian thought (such as repeatedly quoting <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a> and using a definition from <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>'s <i>Organon</i>) but in an orthodox way. Athanasius was also familiar with the theories of various philosophical schools and in particular with the developments of <a href="/wiki/Neoplatonism" title="Neoplatonism">neoplatonism</a>. Ultimately, Athanasius would modify the philosophical thought of the <a href="/wiki/Catechetical_School_of_Alexandria" title="Catechetical School of Alexandria">School of Alexandria</a> away from the Origenist principles such as the "entirely allegorical interpretation of the text". Still, in later works, Athanasius quotes <a href="/wiki/Homer" title="Homer">Homer</a> more than once (<i>Hist. Ar. 68, Orat. iv. 29</i>).
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG/170px-Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG/255px-Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG/340px-Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1106" data-file-height="1467" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_Frederikskirken.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>St. Athanasius</i> (1883–84), by <a href="/wiki/Carl_Rohl-Smith" title="Carl Rohl-Smith">Carl Rohl-Smith</a>, <a href="/wiki/Frederik%27s_Church" title="Frederik&#39;s Church">Frederik's Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Copenhagen,_Denmark" class="mw-redirect" title="Copenhagen, Denmark">Copenhagen, Denmark</a></div></div></div>
<p>Athanasius knew Greek and admitted not knowing <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> [see, e.g., the 39th Festal Letter of St. Athan]. The Old Testament passages he quotes frequently come from the <a href="/wiki/Septuagint" title="Septuagint">Septuagint</a> Greek translation. Only rarely did he use other Greek versions (to <a href="/wiki/Aquila_of_Sinope" title="Aquila of Sinope">Aquila</a> once in the <i>Ecthesis</i>, to other versions once or twice on the Psalms), and his knowledge of the Old Testament was limited to the Septuagint.<sup id="cite_ref-GK_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GK-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#What_information_to_include" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="A complete citation is needed. (May 2021)">full citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The combination of Scriptural study and of Greek learning was characteristic of the famous Alexandrian School.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Bishop (or Patriarch, the highest ecclesial rank in the Centre of the Church, in Alexandria) Alexander ordained Athanasius a deacon in 319.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (May 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In 325, Athanasius served as Alexander's secretary at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a>. Already a recognized theologian and <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a>, he was the obvious choice to replace his ageing mentor Alexander as the <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">Patriarch of Alexandria</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> despite the opposition of the followers of <a href="/wiki/Arius" title="Arius">Arius</a> and <a href="/wiki/Melitius_of_Lycopolis" title="Melitius of Lycopolis">Meletius of Lycopolis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At length, in the Council of Nicaea, the term "consubstantial" (<a href="/wiki/Homoousion" title="Homoousion">homoousion</a>) was adopted, and a formulary of faith embodying it was drawn up by <a href="/wiki/Hosius_of_Corduba" title="Hosius of Corduba">Hosius of Córdoba</a>. From this time to the end of the Arian controversies, the word "consubstantial" continued to be the test of orthodoxy. The formulary of faith drawn up by Hosius is known as the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 232">&#58;&#8202;232&#8202;</span></sup> However, "he was not the originator of the famous 'homoousion' (<small><a href="/wiki/Accusative_case" title="Accusative case">ACC</a></small> of <i>homoousios</i>). The term had been proposed in a non-obvious and illegitimate sense by <a href="/wiki/Paul_of_Samosata" title="Paul of Samosata">Paul of Samosata</a> to the Fathers at Antioch, and had been rejected by them as savouring of materialistic conceptions of the Godhead."<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While still a deacon under Alexander's care (or early in his patriarchate as discussed below) Athanasius may have also become acquainted with some of the solitaries of the Egyptian desert, and in particular <a href="/wiki/Anthony_the_Great" title="Anthony the Great">Anthony the Great</a>, whose life he is said to have written.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1930_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1930-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_to_Arianism">Opposition to Arianism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Opposition to Arianism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Arian_controversy" title="Arian controversy">Arian controversy</a></div>
<p>In about 319, when Athanasius was a deacon, a <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyter</a> named Arius came into a direct conflict with Alexander of Alexandria. It appears that Arius reproached Alexander for what he felt were misguided or heretical teachings being taught by the bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Arius' theological views appear to have been firmly rooted in Alexandrian Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He embraced a subordinationist <a href="/wiki/Christology" title="Christology">Christology</a> which taught that Christ was the divine Son (<a href="/wiki/Logos_(Christianity)" title="Logos (Christianity)">Logos</a>) of God, made, not begotten. This view was heavily influenced by Alexandrian thinkers like Origen<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> and was a common Christological view in Alexandria at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Arius had support from a powerful bishop named <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Nicomedia" title="Eusebius of Nicomedia">Eusebius of Nicomedia</a> (not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> of Caesarea),<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> illustrating how Arius's subordinationist Christology was shared by other Christians in the empire. Arius was subsequently excommunicated by Alexander, and Arius began to elicit the support of many bishops who agreed with his position.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 297">&#58;&#8202;297&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patriarch">Patriarch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Patriarch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: plum;"><span style="font-size:80%">Papal styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Pope Athanasius I</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Coptic_cross.svg" class="image"><img alt="Coptic cross.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Coptic_cross.svg/75px-Coptic_cross.svg.png" decoding="async" width="75" height="75" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Coptic_cross.svg/113px-Coptic_cross.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Coptic_cross.svg/150px-Coptic_cross.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="606" data-file-height="606" /></a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Holiness" class="mw-redirect" title="His Holiness">His Holiness</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Holiness</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religious style</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Pope_(word)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope (word)">Pope</a> and <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">Patriarch</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Posthumous style</th><td class="infobox-data">Saint</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Frances A. M. Forbes writes that when Patriarch Alexander was on his death-bed he called Athanasius, who fled fearing he would be constrained to be made bishop. "When the Bishops of the Church assembled to elect their new Patriarch, the whole Catholic population surrounded the church, holding up their hands to Heaven and crying; "Give us Athanasius!" The Bishops had nothing better. Athanasius was thus elected, as Gregory tells us..." (<a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">Pope Gregory I</a> had full access to the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Apostolic_Archive" title="Vatican Apostolic Archive">Vatican Archives</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: Chapter 4">&#58;&#8202;Chapter 4&#8202;</span></sup> Alban Butler writes on the subject: "Five months after this great Council, Nicae, St Alexander lying on his deathbed, recommended to his clergy and people the choice of Athanasius for his successor, thrice repeating his name. In consequence of his recommendation, the bishops of all Egypt assembled at Alexandria, and finding the people and clergy unanimous in their choice of Athanasius for patriarch, they confirmed the election about the middle of year 326. He seems, then, to have been about thirty years of age."<sup id="cite_ref-Butler1860_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler1860-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>T. Gilmartin (Professor of History, <a href="/wiki/Maynooth" title="Maynooth">Maynooth</a>, 1890) writes: "On the death of Alexander, five months after the termination of the Council of Nicaea, Athanasius was unanimously elected to fill the vacant see. He was most unwilling to accept the dignity, for he clearly foresaw the difficulties in which it would involve him. The clergy and people were determined to have him as their bishop, Patriarch of Alexandria, and refused to accept any excuses. He at length consented to accept a responsibility that he sought in vain to escape, and was consecrated in 326, when he was about thirty years of age."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 244–248">&#58;&#8202;244–248&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Athanasius' episcopate began on 9 May 328 as the <a href="/wiki/Councils_of_Alexandria" title="Councils of Alexandria">Alexandrian Council</a> elected Athanasius to succeed after the death of Alexander.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 245">&#58;&#8202;245&#8202;</span></sup> Patriarch Athanasius spent over 17 years in five exiles ordered by four different Roman Emperors, not counting approximately six more incidents in which Athanasius fled Alexandria to escape people seeking to take his life.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> During his first years as bishop, Athanasius visited the churches of his territory, which at that time included all of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Egypt" title="Roman Egypt">Egypt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Libya_in_the_Roman_era" title="Libya in the Roman era">Libya</a>. He established contacts with the hermits and monks of the desert, including <a href="/wiki/Pachomius_the_Great" title="Pachomius the Great">Pachomius</a>, which proved very valuable to him over the years.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>"During the forty-eight years of his episcopate, his history is told in the history of the controversies in which he was constantly engaged with the Arians, and of the sufferings he had to endure in defence of the Nicene faith. 
We have seen that when Arius was allowed to return from exile in 328, Athanasius refused to remove the sentence of excommunication."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page: 245">&#58;&#8202;245&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="First_exile">First exile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: First exile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Athanasius' first problem lay with Meletius of Lycopolis and his followers, who had failed to abide by the First Council of Nicaea. That council also <a href="/wiki/Anathema" title="Anathema">anathematized</a> Arius. Accused of mistreating Arians and Meletians, Athanasius answered those charges at a gathering of bishops at the <a href="/wiki/First_Synod_of_Tyre" title="First Synod of Tyre">First Synod of Tyre</a> in 335. There, Eusebius of Nicomedia and other supporters of Arius deposed Athanasius.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (May 2022)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> On 6 November, both sides of the dispute met with Emperor <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine I</a> in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> At that meeting, the Arians claimed Athanasius would try to cut off essential Egyptian grain supplies to Constantinople. He was found guilty and sent into exile to Augusta Treverorum in Gaul (now <a href="/wiki/Trier" title="Trier">Trier</a> in Germany).<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (May 2022)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Christianity,_Daily_Telegraph_1999_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Christianity,_Daily_Telegraph_1999-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#What_information_to_include" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="A complete citation is needed. (May 2022)">full citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>When Athanasius reached his destination in exile in 336, <a href="/wiki/Maximin_of_Trier" title="Maximin of Trier">Maximin of Trier</a> received him, but not as a disgraced person. Athanasius stayed with him for two years.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler1860_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler1860-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Constantine died in 337 and was succeeded by his three sons, <a href="/wiki/Constantine_II_(emperor)" title="Constantine II (emperor)">Constantine II</a>, <a href="/wiki/Constantius_II" title="Constantius II">Constantius</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Constans" title="Constans">Constans</a>. <a href="/wiki/Paul_I_of_Constantinople" title="Paul I of Constantinople">Paul I of Constantinople</a> had cautioned Emperor Constans against the Arians, revealing their plots, and he also had been banished and found shelter with Maximin.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_exile">Second exile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Second exile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_(south_east_groin-vault)_-_Athanasios.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg/170px-Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="180" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg/255px-Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg/340px-Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_%28south_east_groin-vault%29_-_Athanasios.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1053" data-file-height="1117" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hosios_Loukas_Crypt_(south_east_groin-vault)_-_Athanasios.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Fresco at <a href="/wiki/Hosios_Loukas" title="Hosios Loukas">Hosios Loukas</a>, Greece (11th century)</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg/170px-Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg/255px-Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg/340px-Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2112" data-file-height="2816" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_of_Alexandria.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of the saint in St Athanasius's <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> in <a href="/wiki/Evanston,_Illinois" title="Evanston, Illinois">Evanston, Illinois</a></div></div></div>
<p>When Emperor Constantine I died, Athanasius was allowed to return to his <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">See</a> of Alexandria. Shortly thereafter, however, Constantius II renewed the order for Athanasius's banishment in 338. "Within a few weeks he set out for Rome to lay his case before the Church at large. He had made his appeal to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_I" title="Pope Julius I">Pope Julius</a>, who took up his cause with whole-heartedness that never wavered down to the day of that holy pontiff's death. The pope summoned a synod of bishops to meet in Rome. After a careful and detailed examination of the entire case, the primate's innocence was proclaimed to the Christian world."<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> During this time, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Cappadocia" title="Gregory of Cappadocia">Gregory of Cappadocia</a>, an Arian bishop, was installed as the patriarch of Alexandria, usurping the absent Athanasius. Athanasius did, however, remain in contact with his people through his annual <i>Festal Letters</i>, in which he also announced on which date <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a> would be celebrated that year.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 339 or 340, nearly one hundred bishops met at Alexandria, declared in favour of Athanasius,<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and vigorously rejected the criticisms of the Eusebian faction at Tyre. Plus, Pope Julius wrote to the supporters of Arius strongly urging Athanasius's reinstatement, but that effort proved in vain. Julius called a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> in Rome in 340 to address the matter, which proclaimed Athanasius the rightful bishop of Alexandria.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Early in 343 Athanasius met with Hosius of Córdoba, and together they set out for <a href="/wiki/Sofia" title="Sofia">Serdica</a>. A full council of the Church was summoned there in deference to the Roman pontiff's wishes. At this great gathering of prelates, leaders of the Church, the case of Athanasius was taken up once more, that is, Athanasius was formally questioned over misdemeanours and even murder, (a bishop in Egypt named Arsenius had turned up missing, and they blamed his death on Athanasius, even supposedly producing Arsenius' severed hand.)<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The council was convoked for the purpose of inquiring into the charges against Athanasius and other bishops, on account of which they were deposed from their sees by the semi-Arian Synod of Antioch in 341 and went into exile. Eusebian bishops objected to the admission of Athanasius and other deposed bishops to the council, except as accused persons to answer the charges brought against them. Their objections were overridden by the orthodox bishops. The Eusebians, seeing they had no chance of having their views carried, retired to <a href="/wiki/Philippopolis_(Thrace)" title="Philippopolis (Thrace)">Philoppopolis</a> in Thrace where they held an opposition council under the presidency of the Patriarch of Antioch and confirmed the decrees of the <a href="/wiki/Synods_of_Antioch" title="Synods of Antioch">Synod of Antioch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Pages: 233–234">&#58;&#8202;233–234&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Athanasius' innocence was reaffirmed at the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Serdica" title="Council of Serdica">Council of Serdica</a>. Two conciliar letters were prepared, one to the clergy and faithful of Alexandria, the other to the bishops of Egypt and Libya, in which the will of the council was made known. Meanwhile, the Eusebians issued an anathema against Athanasius and his supporters. The persecution against the orthodox party broke out with renewed vigour, and Constantius was induced to prepare drastic measures against Athanasius and the priests who were devoted to him. Orders were given that if Athanasius attempted to re-enter his see, he should be put to death. Athanasius, accordingly, withdrew from Serdica to <a href="/wiki/Ni%C5%A1" title="Niš">Naissus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mysia" title="Mysia">Mysia</a>, where he celebrated the Easter festival of the year 344.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Hosius presided over the Council of Serdica, as he did for the First Council of Nicaea, which like the 341 synod found Athanasius innocent.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1878_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1878-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He celebrated his last Easter in exile in <a href="/wiki/Aquileia" title="Aquileia">Aquileia</a> in April 345, received by Bishop <a href="/wiki/Fortunatianus_of_Aquileia" title="Fortunatianus of Aquileia">Fortunatianus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Council of Serdica sent an emissary to report their finding to Constantius. Constantius reconsidered his decision, owing to a threatening letter from his brother Constans and the uncertain conditions of affairs on the Persian border, and he accordingly made up his mind to yield. But three separate letters were needed to overcome the natural hesitation of Athanasius. When he finally acquiesced to meet with Constantius, he was accorded a gracious interview by the emperor and sent back to his see in triumph and began ten years of peace.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Julius died in April 352 and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Liberius" title="Pope Liberius">Liberius</a>. For two years Liberius had been favourable to the cause of Athanasius; but driven at last into exile, he was induced to sign an ambiguous formula, from which the great Nicene text, the "homoousion", had been studiously omitted. In 355 a <a href="/wiki/Synod_of_Milan" title="Synod of Milan">council was held at Milan</a>, where in spite of the vigorous opposition of a handful of loyal prelates among the Western bishops, a fourth condemnation of Athanasius was announced to the world. With his friends scattered, Hosius in exile, and Pope Liberius denounced as acquiescing in Arian formularies, Athanasius could hardly hope to escape. On the night of 8 February 356, while engaged in services in the Church of St. Thomas, a band of armed men burst in to secure his arrest. It was the beginning of his third exile.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gilmartin writes: "By Constantius' order, the sole ruler of The Roman Empire at the death of his brother Constans, the <a href="/wiki/Synod_of_Arles" title="Synod of Arles">Council of Arles in 353</a>, was held, which was presided over by Vincent, Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Capua" title="Capua">Capua</a>, in the name of Pope Liberius. The fathers terrified of the threats of the Emperor, an avowed Arian, they consented to the condemnation of Athanasius. The Pope refused to accept their decision, and requested the Emperor to hold another Council, in which the charges against Athanasius could be freely investigated. To this Constantius consented, for he felt able to control the Council in Milan."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 234">&#58;&#8202;234&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In 355, three hundred bishops assembled in Milan, most from the West and only a few from the East. They met in the Church of Milan. Shortly, the emperor ordered them to a hall in the Imperial Palace, thus ending any free debate. He presented an Arian formula of faith for their acceptance. He threatened any who refused with exile and death. All, with the exception of <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_(bishop_of_Milan)" title="Dionysius (bishop of Milan)">Dionysius (bishop of Milan)</a>, and the two Papal Legates, viz., <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Vercelli" title="Eusebius of Vercelli">Eusebius of Vercelli</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lucifer_of_Cagliari" title="Lucifer of Cagliari">Lucifer of Cagliari</a>, consented to the <a href="/wiki/Arian_creeds" title="Arian creeds">Arian Creed</a> and the condemnation of Athanasius. Those who refused were sent into exile. The decrees were forwarded to the pope for approval but were rejected because of the violence to which the bishops were subjected.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 235">&#58;&#8202;235&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Third_exile">Third exile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Third exile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Athanasius.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Saint_Athanasius.jpg/220px-Saint_Athanasius.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Saint_Athanasius.jpg/330px-Saint_Athanasius.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Saint_Athanasius.jpg/440px-Saint_Athanasius.jpg 2x" data-file-width="459" data-file-height="309" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Athanasius.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Athanasius at the Council of Nicea, <a href="/wiki/William_of_Tyre" title="William of Tyre">William of Tyre</a> manuscripts</div></div></div>
<p>Through the influence of the Eusebian faction at Constantinople, an Arian bishop, <a href="/wiki/George_of_Cappadocia" title="George of Cappadocia">George of Cappadocia</a>, was appointed to rule the see of Alexandria in 356. Athanasius, after remaining some days in the neighbourhood of the city, finally withdrew into the desert of <a href="/wiki/Upper_Egypt" title="Upper Egypt">Upper Egypt</a> where he remained for a period of six years, living the life of the monks and devoting himself to the composition of a group of writings, such as his <i>Letter to the Monks</i> and <i>Four Orations against the Arians</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He also defended his own recent conduct in the <i>Apology to Constantius</i> and <i>Apology for His Flight</i>. Constantius' persistence in his opposition to Athanasius, combined with reports Athanasius received about the persecution of non-Arians by the Arian bishop <a href="/wiki/George_of_Laodicea" title="George of Laodicea">George of Laodicea</a>, prompted Athanasius to write his more emotional <i>History of the Arians</i>, in which he described Constantius as a precursor of the <a href="/wiki/Antichrist" title="Antichrist">Antichrist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Constantius died on 4 November 361 and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Julian_(emperor)" title="Julian (emperor)">Julian</a>. The proclamation of the new prince's accession was the signal for a pagan outbreak against the still dominant Arian faction in Alexandria. George, the usurping bishop, was imprisoned and murdered. An obscure presbyter named Pistus was chosen by the Arians to succeed him, when news arrived that filled the orthodox party with hope. An edict had been put forth by Julian permitting the exiled bishops of the "Galileans" to return to their "towns and provinces". Athanasius accordingly returned to Alexandria on 22 February 362.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 362 Athanasius convened a council at Alexandria and presided over it with <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Vercelli" title="Eusebius of Vercelli">Eusebius of Vercelli</a>. Athanasius appealed for unity among all those who had faith in Christianity, even if they differed on matters of terminology. This prepared the groundwork for his definition of the orthodox doctrine of the <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinity</a>. However, the council also was directed against those who denied the divinity of the Holy Spirit, the human soul of Christ, and Christ's divinity. Mild measures were agreed on for those heretic bishops who repented, but severe penance was decreed for the chief leaders of the major heresies.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With characteristic energy he set to work to re-establish the somewhat shattered fortunes of the orthodox party and to purge the theological atmosphere of uncertainty. To clear up the misunderstandings that had arisen in the course of the previous years, an attempt was made to determine still further the significance of the Nicene formularies. In the meanwhile, Julian, who seems to have become suddenly jealous of the influence that Athanasius was exercising at Alexandria, addressed an order to Ecdicius, the Prefect of Egypt, peremptorily commanding the expulsion of the restored primate on the ground that he had not been included in the imperial act of clemency. The edict was communicated to the bishop by Pythicodorus Trico, who, though described in the "Chronicon Athanasianum" (XXXV) as a "philosopher", seems to have behaved with brutal insolence. On 23 October the people gathered about the proscribed bishop to protest against the emperor's decree; but Athanasius urged them to submit, consoling them with the promise that his absence would be of short duration.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Fourth_exile">Fourth exile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Fourth exile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 362 Julian, noted for his opposition to Christianity, ordered Athanasius to leave Alexandria once again. Athanasius left for Upper Egypt, remaining there with the <a href="/wiki/Desert_Fathers" title="Desert Fathers">Desert Fathers</a> until Julian's death on 26 June 363. Athanasius returned in secret to Alexandria, where he received a document from the new emperor, <a href="/wiki/Jovian_(emperor)" title="Jovian (emperor)">Jovian</a>, reinstating him once more in his episcopal functions.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> 
His first act was to convene a council which reaffirmed the terms of the Nicene Creed. Early in September 363 he set out for <a href="/wiki/Antioch_on_the_Orontes" class="mw-redirect" title="Antioch on the Orontes">Antioch on the Orontes</a>, bearing a synodal letter, in which the pronouncements of this council had been embodied. At Antioch he had an interview with Jovian, who received him graciously and even asked him to prepare an exposition of the orthodox faith. In February 364 Jovian died.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Fifth_exile">Fifth exile</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Fifth exile">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The accession of Emperor <a href="/wiki/Valens" title="Valens">Valens</a> gave a fresh lease of life to the Arian party. He issued a decree banishing the bishops who had been deposed by Constantius but who had been permitted by Jovian to return to their sees. The news created the greatest consternation in Alexandria, and the prefect, in order to prevent a serious outbreak, gave public assurance that the very special case of Athanasius would be laid before the emperor. But Athanasius seems to have divined what was preparing in secret against him. He quietly withdrew from Alexandria in October 364 and took up his abode in a country house outside the city. Valens, who seems to have sincerely dreaded the possible consequences of another popular outbreak, within a few weeks issued orders allowing Athanasius to return to his <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">episcopal see</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Some early reports state that Athanasius spent this period of exile at his family's ancestral tomb<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> in a Christian cemetery. 
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_and_death">Final years and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Final years and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>After returning to Alexandria, Athanasius spent his final years repairing all the damage done during the earlier years of violence, dissent, and exile. He resumed writing and preaching undisturbed, and characteristically re-emphasized the view of the <a href="/wiki/Incarnation_(Christianity)" title="Incarnation (Christianity)">Incarnation</a> which had been defined at Nicaea. On 2 May 373, having consecrated <a href="/wiki/Pope_Peter_II_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Peter II of Alexandria">Peter II</a>, one of his presbyters as his successor, Athanasius died peacefully in his own bed, surrounded by his clergy and faithful supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1930_14-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1930-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Coptic_literature" title="Coptic literature">Coptic literature</a>, Athanasius is the first patriarch of Alexandria to use <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> as well as Greek in his writings.<sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Polemical_and_theological_works">Polemical and theological works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Polemical and theological works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Athanasius was not a speculative theologian. As he states in his <i>First Letters to Serapion</i>, he held on to "the tradition, teaching, and faith proclaimed by the apostles and guarded by the fathers."<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He held that both the Son of God and the Holy Spirit are <a href="/wiki/Consubstantiality" title="Consubstantiality">consubstantial</a> with the Father, which had a great deal of influence in the development of later doctrines regarding the Trinity.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius' <i>"Letter Concerning the Decrees of the Council of Nicaea"</i> (<i>De Decretis</i>), is an important historical as well as theological account of the proceedings of that council.
</p><p>Examples of Athanasius' polemical writings against his theological opponents include <i>Orations Against the Arians</i>, his defence of the divinity of the Holy Spirit (<i>Letters to Serapion</i> in the 360s, and <i>On the Holy Spirit</i>), against <a href="/wiki/Pneumatomachi" title="Pneumatomachi">Macedonianism</a> and <i>On the Incarnation</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius also authored a two-part work, <i>Against the Heathen</i> and <i>The Incarnation of the Word of God</i>. Completed probably early in his life, before the Arian controversy,<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> they constitute the first classic work of developed Orthodox theology. In the first part, Athanasius attacks several pagan practices and beliefs. The second part presents teachings on the redemption.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Also in these books, Athanasius put forward the belief, referencing <span class="nourlexpansion nowrap"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1%3A1%E2%80%934&amp;version=NRSV">John 1:1–4</a></span>, that the Son of God, the eternal Word (Logos) through whom God created the world, entered that world in human form to lead men back into the harmony from which they had earlier fallen away.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>His other important works include his <i>Letters to Serapion</i>, which defends the <a href="/wiki/Divinity" title="Divinity">divinity</a> of the Holy Spirit. In a letter to Epictetus of Corinth, Athanasius anticipates future controversies in his defence of the humanity of Christ. In a letter addressed to the monk Dracontius, Athanasius urges him to leave the desert for the more active duties of a bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius also wrote several works of <a href="/wiki/Biblical_criticism" title="Biblical criticism">Biblical exegesis</a>, primarily on <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a> materials. The most important of these is his <i>Epistle to Marcellinus</i> (PG 27:12–45) on how to incorporate psalm-saying into one's spiritual practice. Excerpts remain of his discussions concerning the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Book of Genesis</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Song_of_Songs" title="Song of Songs">Song of Solomon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Psalms" title="Psalms">Psalms</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>
Perhaps his most notable letter was his Festal Letter, written to his Church in Alexandria when he was in exile, as he could not be in their presence. This letter clearly shows his stand that accepting Jesus as the Divine Son of God is not optional but necessary: <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I know moreover that not only this thing saddens you, but also the fact that while others have obtained the churches by violence, you are meanwhile cast out from your places. For they hold the places, but you the Apostolic Faith. They are, it is true, in the places, but outside of the true Faith; while you are outside the places indeed, but the Faith, within you. Let us consider whether is the greater, the place or the Faith. Clearly the true Faith. Who then has lost more, or who possesses more? He who holds the place, or he who holds the Faith?<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biographical_and_ascetic_works">Biographical and ascetic works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Biographical and ascetic works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>His biography of <a href="/wiki/Anthony_the_Great" title="Anthony the Great">Anthony the Great</a> entitled <i>Life of Antony</i><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>(Βίος καὶ Πολιτεία Πατρὸς Ἀντωνίου, <i>Vita Antonii</i>) became his most widely read work. Translated into several languages, it became something of a best seller in its day and played an important role in the spreading of the <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a> ideal in Eastern and Western Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_18-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> It depicts Anthony as an illiterate yet holy man who continuously engages in spiritual exercises in the Egyptian desert and struggles against demonic powers. It later served as an inspiration to Christian <a href="/wiki/Monasticism" title="Monasticism">monastics</a> in both the East and the West.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius' works on asceticism also include a <i>Discourse on Virginity</i>, a short work on <i>Love and Self-Control</i>, and a treatise <i>On Sickness and Health</i> (of which only fragments remain).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Misattributed_works">Misattributed works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Misattributed works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Apocalypse_of_Pseudo-Athanasius" title="Apocalypse of Pseudo-Athanasius">Apocalypse of Pseudo-Athanasius</a></div>
<p>There are several other works ascribed to him, although not necessarily generally accepted as being his own. These include the so-called <a href="/wiki/Athanasian_Creed" title="Athanasian Creed">Athanasian Creed</a> (which is today generally seen as being of 5th-century Galician origin), and a complete <i>Expositions on the Psalms</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-EA_17-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EA-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Eschatology">Eschatology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Eschatology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Based on his understanding of the prophecies of <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Daniel" title="Book of Daniel">Daniel</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>, Athanasius described Jesus’ <a href="/wiki/Second_Coming" title="Second Coming">Second Coming</a> in the clouds of heaven and pleads with his readers to be ready for that day, at which time Jesus would <a href="/wiki/Last_Judgment" title="Last Judgment">judge the earth</a>, <a href="/wiki/Universal_resurrection" title="Universal resurrection">raise the dead</a>, cast out the wicked, and <a href="/wiki/Millennialism" title="Millennialism">establish his kingdom</a>. Athanasius also argued that the date of Jesus’ earthly sojourn was divinely foretold beyond refutation by the <a href="/wiki/Prophecy_of_Seventy_Weeks" title="Prophecy of Seventy Weeks">seventy weeks prophecy</a> of Daniel 9.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroom1950393_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroom1950393-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Athanasius was originally buried in Alexandria, but his remains were later transferred to the <a href="/wiki/San_Zaccaria,_Venice" title="San Zaccaria, Venice">Chiesa di San Zaccaria</a> in <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>, Italy. During <a href="/wiki/Pope_Shenouda_III_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Shenouda III of Alexandria">Pope Shenouda III</a>'s visit to Rome (4–10 May 1973), <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> gave the Coptic Patriarch a <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relic</a> of Athanasius,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> which he brought back to Egypt on 15 May.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="This reference citation appears to be to a self-published source. (May 2022)">self-published source</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The relic is currently preserved under the new <a href="/wiki/Saint_Mark%27s_Coptic_Orthodox_Cathedral" title="Saint Mark&#39;s Coptic Orthodox Cathedral">Saint Mark's Coptic Orthodox Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cairo" title="Cairo">Cairo</a>. However, the majority of Athanasius's corpse remains in the Venetian church.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="This reference citation appears to be to a self-published source. (May 2022)">self-published source</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>All major Christian denominations which officially recognize saints venerate Athanasius. Western Christians observe his <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> on 2 May, the anniversary of his death. The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> considers Athanasius a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Doctors_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Doctors-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> For Coptic Christians, his feast day is Pashons 7 (now circa 15 May). Eastern Orthodox liturgical calendars remember Athanasius on 18 January.<sup id="cite_ref-PRAVOSLAVIE_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PRAVOSLAVIE-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius is honored on the liturgical calendars of the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 2 May.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> (330–390, also a Doctor of the Church), said: "When I praise Athanasius, virtue itself is my theme: for I name every virtue as often as I mention him who was possessed of all virtues. He was the true pillar of the Church. His life and conduct were the rule of bishops, and his doctrine the rule of the orthodox faith."<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1930_14-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1930-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px"><div style="width: 235px">
