<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Character">Character</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Character">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Historian Cornelius Clifford says in his account: "Athanasius was the greatest champion of Catholic belief on the subject of the Incarnation that the Church has ever known and in his lifetime earned the characteristic title of 'Father of Orthodoxy', by which he has been distinguished ever since."<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Clifford also says: "His career almost personifies a crisis in the history of Christianity; and he may be said rather to have shaped the events in which he took part than to have been shaped by them."<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford1907_1-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford1907-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> St. John Henry Newman describes him as a "principal instrument, after the Apostles, by which the sacred truths of Christianity have been conveyed and secured to the world".<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The greater majority of Church leaders and the emperors fell into support for Arianism, so much so that <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> (340–420) wrote of the period: "The whole world groaned and was amazed to find itself Arian".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_19-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He, Athanasius, even suffered an unjust excommunication from <a href="/wiki/Pope_Liberius" title="Pope Liberius">Pope Liberius</a> who was exiled and leant towards compromise, until he was allowed back to the See of Rome. Athanasius stood virtually alone against the world.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler1860_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler1860-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_significance_and_controversies">Historical significance and controversies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Historical significance and controversies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Testament_canon">New Testament canon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: New Testament canon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Biblical_canon" title="Biblical canon">Biblical canon</a></div>
<p>It was the custom of the bishops of Alexandria to circulate a letter after <a href="/wiki/Epiphany_(holiday)" title="Epiphany (holiday)">Epiphany</a> each year confirming the date of Easter and therefore other moveable feasts. They also took the occasion to discuss other matters. Athanasius wrote forty-five festal letters.<sup id="cite_ref-Thiede_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thiede-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius' <i>39th Festal Letter</i>, written in 367, is widely regarded as a milestone in the evolution of the <a href="/wiki/Biblical_canon#Christian_canons" title="Biblical canon">canon of New Testament books</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Gwynn_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gwynn-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius is the first person to identify the same 27 books of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> that are in use today. Up until then, various similar lists of works to be read in churches were in use. Athanasius compiled the list to resolve questions about such texts as the <i><a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Barnabas" title="Epistle of Barnabas">Epistle of Barnabas</a></i>. Athanasius includes the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Baruch" title="Book of Baruch">Book of Baruch</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Letter_of_Jeremiah" title="Letter of Jeremiah">Letter of Jeremiah</a> and places the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Esther" title="Book of Esther">Book of Esther</a> among the "7 books not in the canon but to be read" along with the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Wisdom" title="Book of Wisdom">Wisdom of Solomon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Sirach" title="Book of Sirach">Book of Sirach</a>, <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Judith" title="Book of Judith">Book of Judith</a>, <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Tobit" title="Book of Tobit">Book of Tobit</a>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Didache" title="Didache">Didache</a></i>, and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Shepherd_of_Hermas" title="The Shepherd of Hermas">The Shepherd of Hermas</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Robt_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robt-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Athanasius' list is similar to the <a href="/wiki/Codex_Vaticanus" title="Codex Vaticanus">Codex Vaticanus</a> in the Vatican Library, probably written in Rome in 340 by Alexandrian scribes for Emperor Constans, during the period of Athanasius' seven-year exile in the city. The establishment of the canon was not a unilateral decision by a bishop in Alexandria but the result of a process of careful investigation and deliberation, as documented in a codex of the Greek Bible and, twenty-seven years later, in his festal letter.<sup id="cite_ref-Thiede_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thiede-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Damasus_I" title="Pope Damasus I">Pope Damasus I</a>, the bishop of Rome in 382, promulgated a list of books which contained a New Testament canon identical to that of Athanasius. A <a href="/wiki/Synod_of_Hippo" title="Synod of Hippo">synod in Hippo</a> in 393 repeated Athanasius' and Damasus' New Testament list (without the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Hebrews" title="Epistle to the Hebrews">Epistle to the Hebrews</a>), and the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Carthage_(397)" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Carthage (397)">Council of Carthage (397)</a> repeated Athanasius' and Damasus' complete New Testament list.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Scholars debate whether Athanasius' list in 367 formed the basis for later lists. Because Athanasius' canon is the closest canon of any of the Church Fathers to the one used by Protestant churches today, many Protestants point to Athanasius as the Father of the Canon.<sup id="cite_ref-Robt_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robt-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Supporters">Supporters</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Supporters">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg/220px-Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="344" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg/330px-Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e8/Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg 2x" data-file-width="332" data-file-height="519" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Athanasius_and_Cyril.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Athanasius (left) and his supporter <a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Alexandria" title="Cyril of Alexandria">Cyril of Alexandria</a>. 17th-century depiction.</div></div></div>
<p>Christian denominations worldwide revere Athanasius as a saint and teacher. They cite his defence of the Christology described in the first chapter of the Gospel of St. John<sup class="nourlexpansion reference"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1%3A1%E2%80%934&amp;version=NRSV">&#91;1:1–4&#93;</a></sup> and his significant theological works (<a href="/wiki/C._S._Lewis" title="C. S. Lewis">C. S. Lewis</a> calls <i>On the Incarnation of the Word of God</i> a "masterpiece")<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> as evidence of his righteousness. They also emphasize his close relationship with Anthony the Great, the ancient monk who was one of the founders of the Christian monastic movement.
</p><p>The Gospel of St. John, and particularly the first chapter, demonstrates the Divinity of Jesus. This Gospel is the greatest support of Athanasius' stand. The Gospel of St. John's first chapter began to be said at the end of Mass, we believe as a result of Athanasius and his life's stand.<sup class="nourlexpansion reference"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1%3A1%E2%80%9314&amp;version=NRSV">&#91;1:1–14&#93;</a></sup> The beginning of John's Gospel was much used as an object of special devotion throughout the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>; the practice of saying it at the altar grew, and eventually <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_V" title="Pope Pius V">Pope Pius V</a> made this practice universal for the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Rite" title="Roman Rite">Roman Rite</a> in his 1570 edition of the <a href="/wiki/Missal" title="Missal">Missal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fortescue1907_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fortescue1907-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> It became a firm custom with exceptions in using another Gospel in use from 1920.<sup id="cite_ref-Pope_Benedict_XV_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pope_Benedict_XV-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Jungmann_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jungmann-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Alexandria" title="Cyril of Alexandria">Cyril of Alexandria</a> (370–444) in the first letter says: "Athanasius is one who can be trusted: he would not say anything that is not in accord with sacred scripture." (Ep 1).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Critics">Critics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Athanasius_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Critics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Throughout most of his career, Athanasius had many detractors. Classics scholar <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Barnes_(classicist)" title="Timothy Barnes (classicist)">Timothy Barnes</a> recounts ancient allegations against Athanasius: from defiling an altar, to selling Church grain that had been meant to feed the poor for his own personal gain, and even violence and murder to suppress dissent.<sup id="cite_ref-wxdxek_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wxdxek-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius used "Arian" to describe both followers of Arius and as a derogatory polemical term for Christians who disagreed with his formulation of the Trinity.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Athanasius called many of his opponents "Arian", except for <a href="/wiki/Meletius_of_Antioch" title="Meletius of Antioch">Meletius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Scholars now believe that the Arian party was not monolithic<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> but held drastically different theological views that spanned the early Christian theological spectrum.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> They supported the tenets of <a href="/wiki/Origenism" title="Origenism">Origenist</a> thought and <a href="/wiki/Subordinationism" title="Subordinationism">subordinationist</a> theology<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> but had little else in common. Moreover, many labelled "Arian" did not consider themselves followers of Arius.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In addition, non-homoousian bishops disagreed with being labeled as followers of Arius, since Arius was merely a presbyter, while they were fully ordained bishops.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (May 2022)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The old allegations continue to be made against Athanasius, however, many centuries later. For example, <a href="/wiki/Richard_E._Rubenstein" title="Richard E. Rubenstein">Richard E. Rubenstein</a> suggests that Athanasius ascended to the rank of bishop in Alexandria under questionable circumstances because some questioned whether he had reached the minimum age of 30 years, and further that Athanasius employed force when it suited his cause or personal interests. Thus, he argues that a small number of bishops who supported Athanasius held a private consecration to make him bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
